Hmm, but let me think more carefully. "No predicate meaning X appears anywhere" vs. "No predicate meaning X is taken as primitive, and no definition depends on one." Are these really different in what they claim?

"Appears anywhere" in the old wording — in context, this is Part 0's summary of what the theory claims. The theory is about formal predicates (things in the formal semantics). "Appears anywhere" means no such predicate is part of the theory's formal apparatus. "Taken as primitive" means not among the base definitions. Wait, actually, can something appear in the formal apparatus without being primitive or depended on by a definition? But actually, "no definition depends on one" is about definitions depending on a predicate meaning X. If a definition DEFINES such a predicate, then the definition produces one but doesn't depend on one. And other definitions might depend on the defined one. So "no definition depends on one" would be violated if any definition uses the defined predicate.

This is getting complicated. Let me think about it differently. The old wording makes a strong negative claim: such predicates don't exist in the theory. The new wording makes a weaker claim: they're not primitive and aren't needed by definitions. The difference is real: the new wording permits such predicates to exist as derived notions (defined, but not needed by other definitions). But does any definition in the text define such a predicate? No. So in the actual theory, the two wordings are equivalent in truth value. A reader of the new wording can conclude that the theory is compatible with derived "explains" predicates, as long as definitions don't depend on them. **R18** - Part V, "What (E) excludes, and what it does not", line 269. CLAIM, clarification.

Declaration: "Part V no longer says outright that a table encoding an organization's response to every admitted change is an account: it satisfies (F1), and it is an account when it meets the other conjuncts of (E)."

The new wording says: the table satisfies (F1) AND it is an account WHEN it meets the other conjuncts of (E). This is conditional: the table is an account only if it also meets (A), NonCircular, and NonVacuous.

**R45** - Part XIV, Declared inputs, line 516. CLAIM, erratum and clarification.

Declaration: "Part XIV now does three things. It lists among the declared inputs a weighting of credit among several contributions to one achievement, beyond any division its history contains. It applies its rule for a missing input to the normative relation where a claim invokes worth. And it says that a claim needing a probability of truth, a merit function or a ranking of thinkers is unsettled."

This is a big change with three parts. Let me analyze each.

Old: "**Declared inputs.** Besides the two primitives, some claims take stated inputs that the semantics records and does not supply: the obligations \(O\) and \(P\) of a repair, with the occasions each covers (Part XI); the scope of a contract and what makes a restriction appropriate (Part III); the system boundary and continuity of an attribution (Part XII). A verdict that depends on one of these is a verdict given the input; where the input is missing, the verdict is unsettled and the semantics says so rather than choosing the input from the verdict wanted."

New: "**Declared inputs.** Besides the two primitives, some claims take stated inputs that the semantics records and does not supply: the obligations \(O\) and \(P\) of a repair, with the occasions each covers (Part XI); the scope of a contract and what makes a restriction appropriate (Part III); the system boundary and continuity of an attribution (Part XII); a weighting of credit among several contributions to one achievement, beyond any division of credit its history contains (Part XI). A verdict that depends on one of these, or on the normative relation where a claim invokes worth, is a verdict given the input; where the input is missing, the verdict is unsettled and the semantics says so rather than choosing the input from the verdict wanted. The semantics supplies no probability of truth, no merit function and no ranking of thinkers, and a claim that needs one is unsettled."

Let me identify the three changes:
1. Added "a weighting of credit among several contributions to one achievement, beyond any division of credit its history contains (Part XI)" to the list of declared inputs.
2. Changed "A verdict that depends on one of these is a verdict given the input" to "A verdict that depends on one of these, or on the normative relation where a claim invokes worth, is a verdict given the input."
3. Added "The semantics supplies no probability of truth, no merit function and no ranking of thinkers, and a claim that needs one is unsettled."

The declaration says these three things. Let me verify.

The declaration says "It lists among the declared inputs a weighting of credit among several contributions to one achievement, beyond any division its history contains." The declaration slightly abbreviates but is accurate.

(2) The new wording adds "or on the normative relation where a claim invokes worth" to the rule for verdicts. The declaration says "It applies its rule for a missing input to the normative relation where a claim invokes worth." The new wording says "A verdict that depends on one of these, or on the normative relation where a claim invokes worth, is a verdict given the input; where the input is missing, the verdict is unsettled." The sentence says "A verdict that depends on one of these [declared inputs], or on the normative relation... is a verdict given the input." Here "the input" is ambiguous: is it the declared input or N? The sentence treats both as "the input" for the purpose of the rule. But N is not a declared input.

**R46** - Part XIV, Indices, not primitives, line 518. CLAIM, erratum.

Declaration: "Part XIV now separates an index, which is what a claim is relative to, from a declared input, which is something a claim takes as stated and the semantics records and does not supply. It says that the boundary and continuity of an attribution, and the scope a claim states for its contract, are both."

Old: "**Indices, not primitives.** Grain \(\ell\), boundary \(\beta\), continuity \(\Omega\), and the contract \(C\) are declared indices. Every claim is relative to them; none is a predicate that could be true or false."

New: Same + "An index is what a claim is relative to; a declared input is something a claim takes as stated, which the semantics records and does not supply. The boundary and continuity of an attribution, and the scope a claim states for its contract, are values of indices and are declared inputs (above)."

The declaration says two things:
1. Part XIV now separates an index (what a claim is relative to) from a declared input (what a claim takes as stated).
2. It says boundary, continuity, and scope are both (indices and declared inputs).

Let me verify.

(1) "An index is what a claim is relative to; a declared input is something a claim takes as stated, which the semantics records and does not supply." — This defines both terms and distinguishes them. The declaration says "separates an index... from a declared input." Yes, the new wording distinguishes them. But does it "separate" them? It defines both and shows they're different. "Separate" might mean "distinguishes" or "keeps apart." The new wording defines both and says some things are both. So it distinguishes the concepts but says some things belong to both categories. This is "separating" the concepts while allowing overlap in instances. The declaration says "separates an index... from a declared input" — this is about the concepts, not the instances. The new wording does separate the concepts (defines them differently). Good.

(2) "The boundary and continuity of an attribution, and the scope a claim states for its contract, are values of indices and are declared inputs (above)." — This says boundary (β), continuity (Ω), and scope (contract C) are both index values and declared inputs. The declaration says "the boundary and continuity of an attribution, and the scope a claim states for its contract, are both." "Both" means both indices and declared inputs. The new wording says "are values of indices and are declared inputs." So they're both. Accurate.

But wait — the new wording says "the scope a claim states for its contract." The contract C is an index (listed in the old wording). The "scope" is what the contract C represents. But C is a set of edit-boundary pairs. The "scope" is the range of admitted changes. Are these the same? Part III says "The **contract** \(C\subseteq A\times B\) is the set of admitted edit–boundary pairs the claim ranges over." So C IS the scope. "The scope a claim states for its contract" — this is the content of C. C is an index. The scope (what C contains) is the value of the index. And "what makes a restriction appropriate" (from Part XIV's declared inputs list) is about why C is restricted. So "the scope a claim states for its contract" is the index value (C itself), while "what makes a restriction appropriate" is a declared input (the ground for the restriction).

The new wording says "the scope a claim states for its contract" is "values of indices and are declared inputs." So the scope (C) is both an index value and a declared input. But Part XIV's declared inputs list includes "the scope of a contract and what makes a restriction appropriate (Part III)." So "the scope of a contract" is listed as a declared input. And C is an index. So the scope is both. This is what the new wording says.

But is this correct? An index is "what a claim is relative to." A claim is relative to C (the contract). C is an index. A declared input is "something a claim takes as stated, which the semantics records and does not supply." The scope of C (which changes are admitted) is something the claim takes as stated (the modeller states the scope). So it's a declared input. And C is an index (the claim is relative to it). So the scope is both. This is coherent.

But there's a subtlety: C is a mathematical object (a set of pairs). The "scope" is the content of C (what it admits). Are these the same? C IS the scope — the contract defines the scope. So "the scope a claim states for its contract" is C. And C is an index. So the scope is the index value. And it's also a declared input (the modeller states it). This is what the new wording says.

Now, let me check coherence. The new wording says "An index is what a claim is relative to; a declared input is something a claim takes as stated, which the semantics records and does not supply." But the old wording (unchanged part) says "Every claim is relative to them; none is a predicate that could be true or false." This says indices are not predicates. The new wording says "a declared input is something a claim takes as stated." Are declared inputs predicates? The old wording says indices are not predicates. The new wording doesn't say whether declared inputs are predicates. But declared inputs are things like "the obligations O and P" and "the system boundary" — these are data, not predicates. So they're not predicates either. Fine.

But the new wording says "the boundary and continuity of an attribution, and the scope a claim states for its contract, are values of indices and are declared inputs (above)." The "(above)" pointer — does it point to the right place? The "Declared inputs" section is ABOVE the "Indices, not primitives" section (since the order is: Primitives → derived → Declared inputs → Indices → Dependence order → Membership). 1. **Primitives.** (lines 510-514)

4. **Indices, not primitives.** (line 518, where R46 is)

So "Declared inputs" is at line 516, which is ABOVE "Indices, not primitives" at line 518. The "(above)" pointer in R46's new wording points above — to the "Declared inputs" section. This is correct.

But R45's new wording is at line 516 (Declared inputs), and R46's new wording is at line 518 (Indices). R46 says "are declared inputs (above)" — pointing to R45's section. R45 lists "the scope of a contract and what makes a restriction appropriate (Part III)" and "the system boundary and continuity of an attribution (Part XII)" as declared inputs. R46 says "the boundary and continuity of an attribution, and the scope a claim states for its contract, are values of indices and are declared inputs (above)." The "(above)" points to R45's list, which includes these items. So the pointer is correct.

But R45 lists "the scope of a contract and what makes a restriction appropriate" as one item. R46 mentions "the scope a claim states for its contract" as being both an index and a declared input. But "what makes a restriction appropriate" is NOT mentioned in R46's new wording. Is "what makes a restriction appropriate" also both an index and a declared input? "What makes a restriction appropriate" is not an index — it's the ground for the restriction. It's a declared input but not an index. R46 correctly omits it (since it's not an index). Good.

But "the scope of a contract" (R45) vs. "the scope a claim states for its contract" (R46) — are these the same? "The scope of a contract" is the content of C. "The scope a claim states for its contract" is also the content of C. These are the same. Good.

Now, does R46 claim more or less than the declaration? The declaration says "Part XIV now separates an index... from a declared input... It says that the boundary and continuity of an attribution, and the scope a claim states for its contract, are both." The new wording says exactly this. But does the new wording claim anything extra?

The new wording says "An index is what a claim is relative to" — this is a definition of "index." The old wording says "Every claim is relative to them" — this implies indices are what claims are relative to. So the new wording's definition of "index" follows from the old wording. Is this a new claim? No — it's a clarification of what the old wording already implied.

The new wording says "a declared input is something a claim takes as stated, which the semantics records and does not supply." This is a definition of "declared input." The old wording doesn't define "declared input." Part XIV's "Declared inputs" section says "some claims take stated inputs that the semantics records and does not supply." So the definition is consistent with Part XIV. But the "Indices" section didn't previously define "declared input." R46 adds this definition. Is this a new claim? It's a definition — it clarifies what "declared input" means. But it's also a claim about what declared inputs ARE (things a claim takes as stated). The old text didn't state this in the "Indices" section. But Part XIV's "Declared inputs" section did. So R46 is restating what Part XIV already says. This is a clarification, not a new claim.

But the declaration says R46 "separates an index... from a declared input." The old text didn't explicitly separate them — it just listed indices. The new text defines both and distinguishes them. This IS a new claim: the distinction between indices and declared inputs is now explicit. The declaration accounts for this.

Now, does R46's definition of "declared input" conflict with Part XIV's? Part XIV: "some claims take stated inputs that the semantics records and does not supply." R46: "a declared input is something a claim takes as stated, which the semantics records and does not supply." These match. Good.

Does R46's definition of "index" conflict with the old wording? Old: "Every claim is relative to them; none is a predicate that could be true or false." R46: "An index is what a claim is relative to." These are consistent (indices are what claims are relative to). Good.

R46 seems fine. Let me check (d) — verdict moves. R46 is listed for O1, O5, O8, O21, N11, N13, N15, N21.

R46 is about the distinction between indices and declared inputs. This is a meta-level clarification. It shouldn't affect verdicts on specific cases. But let me check a couple.

N21 (the order of adjectives): "She knows it, tacitly. She formed her grasp of it herself." The question is about tacit knowledge and construction. R46 is about indices vs. declared inputs. These don't intersect. No verdict move.

N13 (the parrot): "The parrot has acquired the sounds... not his ideas." This is about representation and relay. R46 is about indices/inputs. No intersection. No verdict move.

O1 (the forecaster): The verdict is about Bruno's retreat. R46 is about indices/inputs. The retreat involves contract narrowing (changing the contract C, which is an index). But R46 doesn't change how contracts work — it just clarifies the distinction between indices and inputs. No verdict move.

I think R46 doesn't move verdicts. But wait — R46 says "the scope a claim states for its contract" is a declared input. This means the scope is something the claim "takes as stated." For O1, Bruno states his scope (months outside the thaw, etc.). The scope is a declared input. The verdict about whether the scope is appropriate would take "what makes a restriction appropriate" as a declared input (from Part XIV). If Bruno states no ground (what makes the restriction appropriate), the verdict on the restriction is unsettled (R10's new wording). But R46 is about the scope being both an index and a declared input — it doesn't change the missing-input rule. R10 handles the missing ground. R46 is about classification. No verdict move from R46 alone.

R46 stands.

**R47** - Part XIV, Dependence order, line 520. CLAIM, erratum.

Declaration: "Part XIV's dependence order now says that the declared indices and the declared inputs depend on nothing in the semantics, because they are stated, not derived."

Old: "**Dependence order.** (O) and (Q) depend on nothing."
New: "**Dependence order.** (O) and (Q) depend on nothing; nor do the declared indices and the declared inputs, which are stated, not derived."

The change adds: "nor do the declared indices and the declared inputs, which are stated, not derived."

The declaration says: "the declared indices and the declared inputs depend on nothing in the semantics, because they are stated, not derived." The new wording says: "nor do the declared indices and the declared inputs, which are stated, not derived." So the new wording says they depend on nothing (like (O) and (Q)), because they are stated, not derived. The declaration is accurate.

But does the new wording claim more or less? The new wording says "nor do the declared indices and the declared inputs" — meaning they also depend on nothing. And "which are stated, not derived" — the reason. The declaration says "depend on nothing in the semantics, because they are stated, not derived." The new wording says "depend on nothing... which are stated, not derived." The word "because" in the declaration implies causation (they depend on nothing BECAUSE they are stated). The new wording uses "which" (a relative clause, providing a reason). These are equivalent — "which are stated, not derived" explains why they depend on nothing. Fine.

Now, coherence. The dependence order lists what depends on what. Adding "nor do the declared indices and the declared inputs" says they're at the base (depend on nothing). But the old wording says "(O) and (Q) depend on nothing" — meaning (O) and (Q) are the base. Adding indices and inputs to the base means there are more base items. This is consistent with R44's new wording ("Everything else is derived from the two primitives, the declared indices and the declared inputs"). If everything is derived from primitives + indices + inputs, then indices and inputs are base items (depend on nothing in the semantics). Good.

But there's a question: the primitives (Θ and N) — do they depend on nothing? The old wording says "(O) and (Q) depend on nothing." (O) is the solution set equation, (Q) is the answer profile. These are defined in terms of the organization D and the query Q. They don't depend on other equations in the semantics — they're the starting points. But the primitives (Θ and N) also depend on nothing — they're primitives. The dependence order starts with "(O) and (Q) depend on nothing" but doesn't mention the primitives. R47 adds indices and inputs but still doesn't mention the primitives.

Is this a problem? The dependence order is about the equations/definitions in the semantics, not about the primitives. (O) and (Q) are the first definitions, and they depend on nothing (within the semantics). The primitives are given (not defined), so they also depend on nothing. But the dependence order is about definitions, not primitives. So starting with "(O) and (Q)" is fine — they're the first definitions.

R47 adds "nor do the declared indices and the declared inputs" — these are also not defined (they're stated). So they depend on nothing. This is coherent.

But wait — the dependence order later says "(K) depends on (O) and a contract." The contract C is an index (and now also a declared input). If C depends on nothing (as R47 says), then (K) depends on (O) and something that depends on nothing. This is fine — (K) depends on (O) and C, both of which are at the base.

But the old dependence order says "(K) depends on (O) and a contract." The contract is an index. If indices depend on nothing (R47), then (K) depends on (O) (which depends on nothing) and C (which depends on nothing). So (K) depends on nothing? No — (K) is defined in terms of (O) and C. (K) DEPENDS on them. (O) and C depend on nothing, but (K) depends on them. The dependence order says what each item depends on. (K) depends on (O) and C. (O) and C depend on nothing. This is a well-founded order. Good.

R47 seems fine. Let me check (d) — verdict moves. R47 is listed for O37. O37 is about circularity in the dependence order. R47 adds that indices and inputs depend on nothing. This is a bookkeeping change and doesn't affect O37's verdict (which is about a circle in the account's internal order, not in the semantics' dependence order). No verdict move.

R47 stands.

**R48** - Part XIV, Dependence order, line 520. CLAIM, erratum.

Declaration: "Part XIV's dependence order now places Ownership on histories and a declared boundary, owned capability on Ownership, (CT1) and a declared continuity, Build on Ownership, and (P) on ProducedBy and the declared obligations with their occasions, and ProducedBy on histories and their active routes."

Old: "Build depends on histories and (E). (N), (G) depend on Deploy and Build. (P), (EK) depend on (G), (E), Deploy."

**R49** - Part XV, "What defeats this class", line 528. CLAIM, clarification.

Declaration: "Part XV now says that a case whose verdict turns on an unstated declared input of Part XIV, or on an unstated normative relation, is a case with a missing input, not a refutation."

Old: "None is protected by notation, by the availability of this document, or by any version label."

New: "None is protected by notation, by the availability of this document, or by any version label. A case whose verdict turns on an input the case does not state, where the input is one of the declared inputs Part XIV lists or the normative relation, is a case with a missing input, not a refutation."

The change adds a sentence. The declaration says Part XV now says that a case whose verdict turns on an unstated declared input or normative relation is a case with a missing input, not a refutation. The new wording says exactly this.

But let me check the declaration against the new wording. The declaration says "an unstated declared input of Part XIV, or on an unstated normative relation." The new wording says "an input the case does not state, where the input is one of the declared inputs Part XIV lists or the normative relation." So the new wording says: the input is not stated by the case, and the input is either a declared input (from Part XIV) or the normative relation. The declaration says "an unstated declared input of Part XIV, or on an unstated normative relation." Match.

But there's a subtle difference: the declaration says "a case whose verdict turns on an unstated declared input" — the input is unstated. The new wording says "a case whose verdict turns on an input the case does not state" — the input is not stated by the case. These are the same: "unstated" = "the case does not state." Fine.

Now, does the new wording claim more or less? The new wording says "is a case with a missing input, not a refutation." The declaration says "is a case with a missing input, not a refutation." Match.

But the new wording also says "where the input is one of the declared inputs Part XIV lists or the normative relation." This is a restriction: the rule only applies to declared inputs and the normative relation. What about other kinds of inputs? The declaration says "an unstated declared input of Part XIV, or on an unstated normative relation" — this matches the restriction. Fine.

Now, coherence. The new wording says "the declared inputs Part XIV lists." Part XIV lists declared inputs including O, P, scope/restriction, boundary/continuity, and weighting of credit. The rule applies to these. And "the normative relation" — which is primitive 2 (not a declared input). So the rule applies to both declared inputs and the normative relation. This is consistent with R45's new wording ("A verdict that depends on one of these, or on the normative relation where a claim invokes worth, is a verdict given the input; where the input is missing, the verdict is unsettled").

R49 says such a case is "a case with a missing input, not a refutation." R45 says the verdict is "unsettled." These are consistent: a missing input makes the verdict unsettled (not a refutation).

But there's a question: does this rule make it too easy to dismiss counterexamples? If any counterexample's verdict turns on an unstated input, it's dismissed as "missing input, not refutation." This could protect the theory from refutation. But the rule is narrow: it only applies to declared inputs and the normative relation. And the case must genuinely need one of these inputs (its verdict turns on one). If the verdict doesn't turn on a declared input, the rule doesn't apply.

For Part XV's attack (A): "A candidate meeting all four conditions of (E) on a physically admitted contract, with a non-declared transport, that plainly provides no account." If such a candidate's assessment turns on a declared input (e.g., the scope of the contract), and the input is not stated, R49 says it's a missing input, not a refutation. But the attack (A) is about a candidate that plainly provides no account — if this is evident without any declared input, R49 doesn't apply.

R49 is about what counts as a refutation. It says cases with missing inputs are not refutations. This is a meta-level rule about Part XV. It doesn't directly change verdicts on situations. But it could change whether a situation counts as a refutation.

For O1 (the forecaster): "Bruno has no explanation of the floods." Is this a refutation of the theory? The theory says an account meets (E) on a contract. The verdict depends on which scope is the right one — which is a declared input (the scope of the contract). If the case doesn't state which scope, the verdict is a missing input.

The verdict "no explanation" is about the overall project (the series is a retreat). This doesn't turn on a specific declared input — it's about the retreat pattern. So R49 doesn't apply.

Hmm, but one could argue: whether the narrowed contracts are "appropriate" is a declared input ("what makes a restriction appropriate"). For O12 (the rota): "Noor found a better question, and its merit is real." This involves merit (worth), which takes N as input. If N is not stated, the verdict is a missing input (R49). Does R49 change O12's verdict? Under the old text: a claim of worth takes N as a declared input. Under the new text (R02, R38, R45): a claim of worth takes N as an input (primitive 2). If N is provided (and says the question has merit), the verdict is "yes." If N is not provided, the verdict is unsettled.

Under the old text (R02's old wording): "Where a claim needs one of these, the semantics takes it as a declared input." So a claim of merit takes a declared input. If the input is provided, the verdict is given the input. If not... the old text doesn't have R45's missing-input rule. Wait, the old text of R45 says: "A verdict that depends on one of these is a verdict given the input; where the input is missing, the verdict is unsettled." So the old text DOES have the missing-input rule (for declared inputs). But the old text doesn't include "the normative relation" in the rule (R45's change adds it). So under the old text, a claim of worth takes N as a declared input (R02's old wording), and if it's missing, the verdict is unsettled (R45's old wording). Under the new text, a claim of worth takes N as an input (primitive), and if it's missing, the verdict is unsettled (R45's new wording + R49).

In both cases, if N is missing, the verdict is unsettled. R49 adds "not a refutation" — so a missing-input case is not a refutation. But this is about refutation (Part XV), not about the verdict on O12. O12's verdict is "its merit is real" — given N. If N is provided, the verdict is the same under both texts. If N is not provided, the verdict is unsettled under both texts.

Now let me compile my findings. Let me rank them by severity.

**Most serious:**

1. **R04 (WORDING → claim change):** The old wording says no predicate meaning "really explains," "is a cause," or "is knowledge" "appears anywhere." The new wording says such predicates are "not taken as primitive" and "no definition depends on one." This is weaker: it allows such predicates to exist as derived notions (defined but not used in other definitions). A reader can no longer conclude that no such predicate exists. This is a claim change declared as WORDING.

Additionally, the new wording's pointer to Derivation 6 is imprecise: Derivation 6's consequence ("There is no residual predicate meaning...") supports the old wording's "appears nowhere" (if "residual" means "existing") better than the new wording's weaker claim. And "no definition depends on one" is supported by Part XIV's dependence order ("Nothing depends on a predicate meaning..."), not by Derivation 6. But the pointer says "(Derivation 6)."

Actually wait — let me reconsider. Derivation 6's Consequence: "There is no residual predicate meaning 'explains,' 'represents,' 'is a cause,' or 'is knowledge.'" If "residual" means "underived" (left over from the derivation), then "no residual predicate" = "no underived predicate." This is compatible with derived predicates existing. The old wording ("appears nowhere") is stronger. The new wording ("not primitive, not depended on") is... let me think. "Not taken as primitive" = "not underived" = "no residual predicate" (if residual = underived). "No definition depends on one" = "no definition uses one." Together: not underived AND not used by any definition. A derived predicate that's not used by any definition would satisfy both. So the new wording allows derived, unused predicates. Derivation 6's consequence ("no residual predicate") rules out underived predicates but allows derived ones. So the new wording is compatible with Derivation 6.

But the old wording ("appears nowhere") rules out ALL predicates (derived or underived). This is stronger than Derivation 6's consequence. So the old wording overclaims relative to Derivation 6. The new wording is more accurate relative to Derivation 6.

So R04's change (from old to new) brings Part 0 in line with Derivation 6. But it changes a claim (from "appears nowhere" to "not primitive, not depended on"). This is a claim change declared as WORDING.

**R04 reconsidered:**

Old: "No predicate meaning 'really explains', 'is a cause' or 'is knowledge' appears anywhere (Derivation 6)."
New: "No predicate meaning 'really explains', 'is a cause' or 'is knowledge' is taken as primitive, and no definition depends on one (Derivation 6)."

The key question: does the new wording say what the old said "in other words"?

Old: such a predicate appears nowhere.
New: such a predicate is not primitive, and no definition depends on one.

"Not primitive and not depended on by definitions" vs. "appears nowhere." Let me think about whether these are equivalent.

If something "appears nowhere," it doesn't exist in the theory. If it's "not primitive and not depended on," it might exist as a derived notion that's defined but not used.

But wait — if it's defined (by some definition), then that definition "produces" it. Does "no definition depends on one" mean no definition USES one, or no definition DEFINES one? "Depends on" typically means "requires" or "uses." A definition that DEFINES "explains" in terms of fidelity doesn't "depend on" "explains" — it depends on fidelity. So "no definition depends on one" means no definition USES "explains" in its definiens. But a definition could DEFINE "explains" without any definition DEPENDING on it.

So "not primitive and not depended on" allows: "explains" is defined (derived) but not used by any other definition. This is compatible with the new wording but NOT with the old ("appears nowhere").

So the new wording is strictly weaker than the old. It's not "the same in other words." It's a weaker claim.

But is there a reading where they're equivalent? If "appears anywhere" means "appears in any definition or as a primitive," then "appears nowhere" = "not primitive and not defined." The new wording says "not primitive and not depended on." "Not defined" vs. "not depended on" — these differ (a predicate can be defined but not depended on). So even under this reading, they're not equivalent.

Unless "no definition depends on one" means "no definition produces or uses one." If "depends on" includes "defines" (i.e., a definition that defines "explains" "depends on" the concept of "explains"), then "no definition depends on one" means no definition defines or uses "explains." This would make "not primitive and not depended on" = "not primitive and not defined and not used" = "appears nowhere." But this reading of "depends on" is unusual.

I think the standard reading of "depends on" is "requires" or "uses," not "defines." So "no definition depends on one" means no definition USES one. This allows it to be defined. So the new wording is weaker.

Therefore, R04 changes a claim (from stronger to weaker) while declared WORDING. This is a defect.

But wait — let me also check: can a reader conclude something new from the new wording? The new wording allows derived predicates meaning X (as long as no definition uses them). The old wording forbids any such predicates. So the new wording permits something the old forbade. A reader can now conclude: "the theory might have a derived 'explains' predicate that's not used in definitions." The old text left this unconcluded? No — the old text FORBADE it ("appears nowhere"). So the new wording allows something the old forbade. But the question is: "Can a reader now conclude something the current text left unconcluded?" The current text (old wording) FORBADE derived "explains" predicates. The new wording ALLOWS them. So a reader can now conclude: "the theory is compatible with derived 'explains' predicates." This is a new conclusion (the old text precluded it).

Also: "or no longer conclude something it allowed?" The old text allowed concluding "no such predicate exists at all." The new text no longer allows this. So a reader can no longer conclude something the old allowed.

Both directions confirm: R04 changes a claim. Declared WORDING — defect.

**R39 reconsidered:**

Old: "declared as a substantive input when aesthetic value is claimed."
New: "taken as a substantive input when aesthetic value is claimed."

The key question: does "declared as" = "taken as" in other words?

"Declared as a substantive input" — N is declared (by the modeller) to be a substantive input.
"Taken as a substantive input" — N is taken (by the semantics) to be a substantive input.

In the text's terminology:
- "Declared" is associated with "declared inputs" (Part XIV), "declared indices" (Part 0, XIV), "declared provenance" (Part IV). "Declared" implies the modeller states/specifies it.
- "Taken" is used in Part XIV: "It is taken as an input and never derived." "Taken" implies the semantics uses it.

These are different: "declared" = modeller's action; "taken" = semantics's action. The change from "declared" to "taken" shifts who provides N (from modeller declaring to semantics taking). This is a claim change.

But in the aesthetic case, N is a specific mathematical relation (N ⊆ A×K×Rsn×V_A). "Declared as" means the modeller provides this relation. "Taken as" means the semantics accepts it as an input. These describe the same process (the modeller provides N and the semantics uses it) but with different emphasis. Is this a claim change?

In the text's specific terminology, "declared input" is a category. "Declared as a substantive input" puts N in that category. "Taken as a substantive input" doesn't (it's a primitive taken as input). The change from "declared" to "taken" removes N from the "declared input" category. This IS a claim change: N is reclassified from "declared input" to "primitive taken as input."

This aligns with R38's change (which explicitly reclassifies N as primitive 2). But R39 is declared WORDING (no claim change). The reclassification is a claim change.

So R39 changes a claim while declared WORDING. Defect.

But one could argue: R39 is just aligning the second mention of N with R38's change (which reclassifies N). R38 is the CLAIM change; R39 is just the wording alignment. But R39 is declared WORDING — it claims to change no claim. If the alignment itself changes the claim (from "declared" to "taken"), then R39 does change a claim, even if it's aligning with R38.

Hmm, but the WORDING type is "the new wording says what the old wording said, in other words." If the old says "declared as" and the new says "taken as," these say different things (in the text's terminology). So it's not WORDING.

I think R39 FALLS (changes a claim declared as WORDING). But it's a minor change (aligning terminology with R38).

Let me now think about whether R39's change could be defended as WORDING. If "declared as a substantive input" is read as "specified as a substantive input" (where "declared" just means "specified/stated"), then "taken as a substantive input" (where "taken" means "accepted as") could be seen as the same thing. In ordinary English, "declared" and "taken" can overlap ("I declare this to be true" ≈ "I take this to be true"). But in the text's specific terminology, "declared" is loaded. So the change from "declared" to "taken" is significant in context.

I think R39 FALLS. But I'll note it's closely tied to R38.

**R02, R45, and the "merit function" question for O12:**

R02's new wording: "a claim of worth or of aesthetic value takes the normative relation as an input... and a claim that needs any of the others is unsettled." "The others" are "a probability of truth, a merit function, a ranking of thinkers." So "a merit function" makes claims unsettled.

R45's new wording: "The semantics supplies no probability of truth, no merit function and no ranking of thinkers, and a claim that needs one is unsettled." Consistent.

O12's verdict: "Noor found a better question, and its merit is real." This uses "merit." The theory says "a claim that needs a merit function... is unsettled." If O12's claim needs a merit function, it's unsettled. But if O12's claim is about worth (N), it takes N as input.

The question is: does "its merit is real" need a merit function? Or is it a worth claim (N)?

"Merit" in O12 is about the question being good (having merit). This is a worth claim. The theory says worth takes N as input. A "merit function" is something else (a function for ranking/scoring). "Its merit is real" doesn't need a function — it needs a judgment of worth (N). So O12's claim takes N as input, not a merit function. Not unsettled.

But "found a better question" — "better" implies comparison. Does comparison need a merit function? The theory says "a ranking of thinkers" is not supplied. "Better question" is a ranking of questions. But "ranking of thinkers" is specific to thinkers. A "merit function" could rank questions. If "better" needs a merit function, it's unsettled.

Hmm, but "better" is a comparative worth claim. If N evaluates worth, then "better" means "higher worth according to N." N can handle comparisons (if N is a relation that ranks). But N is a "normative relation" — it might or might not rank. The text says N is "taken as an input and never derived." It's up to the input to determine what N does. If N ranks, then "better" is expressible via N. If N doesn't rank, "better" needs something else (a merit function).

But the fixed verdict says "Noor found a better question, and its merit is real." This is a positive claim. The theory says it takes N as input (for worth). The verdict is given N. If N says the question has merit and is better, the verdict is "yes." No merit function needed — just N.

But what if someone argues that "better" needs a merit function (for ranking)? Then the verdict is unsettled (R02/R45). The fixed verdict says "merit is real" — a positive claim. The theory says "unsettled" (if a merit function is needed). This would be a verdict difference: theory is silent, verdict is positive.

But I think "merit is real" is a worth claim (N), not a merit function claim. The theory says worth takes N as input. The verdict is given N. No merit function needed.

So R02 and R45 don't move O12's verdict. The distinction between "worth" (N) and "merit function" (not supplied) is important, and O12's claim is about worth (N).

But there's a potential confusion: the word "merit" appears in both "merit is real" (O12's verdict) and "merit function" (not supplied). A reader might conflate them. But the theory distinguishes: worth (N) is supplied as an input; a merit function is not supplied. O12's "merit is real" is about worth (N). Fine.

Actually, hold on. Let me look at R02's declaration more carefully: "a claim of worth or of aesthetic value takes the normative relation as an input, and a claim that needs a probability of truth, a merit function or a ranking of thinkers is unsettled." The declaration says "a claim that needs... a merit function... is unsettled." But what if a claim needs BOTH N (for worth) AND a merit function (for ranking)? The distinction is: what does the claim NEED?

For O12: "its merit is real" needs N (for worth). "Found a better question" might need comparison (N or merit function). If "better" is expressible via N, the claim only needs N. If not, it needs a merit function (unsettled).

I think "better" is a worth comparison, which N can handle (if N is a total preorder). The theory says N is "a normative relation" — it could be a preorder. If N ranks, "better" is expressible. So the claim only needs N. Not unsettled.

But this depends on N's structure (which is an input). If N doesn't rank, "better" needs something else. The theory can't determine this without knowing N. So the claim's status depends on N's properties.

Hmm, this is getting too deep into the weeds. Let me just note that O12's verdict might be affected by the merit function question, and move on.

R18 (Part V): "it is an account when it meets the other conjuncts of (E)." The table satisfies (F1) and is an account when it meets (F2), (A), NonCircular, NonVacuous.

R50 (Part XV): "does not fail (F1); like any new attempt, it is a counterexample only if it fails none of the four and still explains nothing." The table doesn't fail (F1) and is a counterexample only if it meets all four conditions and explains nothing.

These are consistent: both say the table meets (F1) and its status as an account/counterexample depends on the other conditions. R18 says it's an account when it meets the other conjuncts. R50 says it's a counterexample when it meets all four and explains nothing. "Meets all four" (R50) = "meets the other conjuncts" (R18) + (F1). Since (F1) is already met, "meets all four" = "meets the other conjuncts." So R18 and R50 are consistent.

But R50 says "fails none of the four" — the four CONDITIONS (Component fidelity = F1+F2, Question fidelity = A, Non-circular, Non-vacuity). R18 says "the other conjuncts of (E)" — the other CONJUNCTS ((F2), (A), NonCircular, NonVacuous). "Fails none of the four conditions" = meets all four conditions = meets F1+F2, A, NonCircular, NonVacuous. "Meets the other conjuncts" = meets (F2), (A), NonCircular, NonVacuous. Since (F1) is already met (both R18 and R50 say so), "meets all four conditions" = "meets (F1) and the other conjuncts" = "meets the other conjuncts" (since (F1) is already met). So "fails none of the four" (R50) = "meets the other conjuncts" (R18). Consistent.

Good. R50 is consistent with R18.

(P) = Repair_{O,P}(ξ,ξ';Δ) ⟺ ∃o∈O[¬o(ξ) ∧ o(ξ')] ∧ ∀r∈P[r(ξ) ⇒ r(ξ')] ∧ ProducedBy(Δ,ξ,ξ';O).

1. ∃o∈O[¬o(ξ) ∧ o(ξ')]: there exists an obligation o in O that was not met at ξ and is met at ξ'.
2. ∀r∈P[r(ξ) ⇒ r(ξ')]: every protected obligation r in P that held at ξ also holds at ξ'.

Wait, but I want to make sure R39 really does change a claim and isn't just a synonym swap. Let me think about this differently.

"Declared as a substantive input" — in English, "declared as X" means "announced/asserted to be X." "Taken as X" means "accepted/used as X." These can overlap: if you declare something as an input, you take it as an input. And if you take something as an input, you've implicitly declared it.

But in the text's specific terminology:
- "Declared" is a technical term: "declared inputs" (Part XIV), "declared indices" (Part 0, XIV), "declared provenance" (Part IV). Being "declared" places something in a specific category.
- "Taken" is used loosely: Part XIV says "It is taken as an input and never derived." "Taken" just means "used/accepted."

So "declared as a substantive input" places N in the "declared input" category. "Taken as a substantive input" doesn't. The change removes N from the "declared input" category. This is a classification change — a claim change.

But one could argue: "declared as a substantive input" doesn't mean "is a declared input." It means "is declared to be a substantive input." The "substantive input" is the category, not "declared input." "Declared" is the verb (how it comes to be), "substantive input" is the predicate (what it is). So "declared as a substantive input" = "asserted to be a substantive input" and "taken as a substantive input" = "used as a substantive input." The difference is in the verb (asserted vs. used), not the category (both are "substantive inputs"). So no classification change.

The verb describes how N comes to be an input (declared by the modeller vs. taken by the semantics). This is a difference in provenance, not in category.

But in the text, "declared" is loaded. "Declared input" is a specific category. Using "declared as" for N suggests N is in that category. Changing to "taken as" removes this suggestion. Even if the category ("substantive input") is the same, the verb ("declared" vs. "taken") carries classificatory weight in the text's terminology.

I think this is genuinely ambiguous. A careful reader would note the difference between "declared" and "taken" in the text's terminology. But a casual reader might see them as synonyms. The text's specific usage of "declared" (as a technical term) makes the difference significant.

Let me also reconsider R04 one more time. The change is from "appears anywhere" to "is taken as primitive, and no definition depends on one." I've argued this is a claim change (from "exists nowhere" to "not primitive, not depended on"). But let me think about whether "in other words" could apply.

"No predicate meaning X appears anywhere" = "there is no predicate meaning X in the theory."
"No predicate meaning X is taken as primitive, and no definition depends on one" = "no predicate meaning X is primitive, and no definition uses one."

Are these "the same in other words"? The first says no such predicate exists. The second says such predicates aren't primitive and aren't used. If such predicates don't exist, they're certainly not primitive and not used. But if they're not primitive and not used, they might still exist (as derived, unused predicates). So the second is weaker.

For them to be "the same in other words," the second would need to imply the first. "Not primitive and not used" → "doesn't exist"? Only if every predicate in the theory is either primitive or used by a definition. If a predicate is defined but not used by any definition, it exists but is neither primitive nor used. So "not primitive and not used" doesn't imply "doesn't exist." Therefore, the second doesn't imply the first. They're not the same.

But wait — in the theory's construction, are there any predicates that are defined but not used? The theory defines predicates like Rep, Faithful, Account, Deploy, Build, New, Origin, Repair, CreateEK, Can, etc. These are all used by other definitions (or by the membership conditions). If every defined predicate is used somewhere, then "not primitive and not used" implies "not defined and not primitive" = "doesn't exist." In that case, the two wordings would be equivalent.

But is this guaranteed? The theory might define a predicate that's not used anywhere (orphan definition). In a well-constructed theory, this shouldn't happen. But the wordings make different claims: "appears nowhere" rules out orphans, "not primitive and not depended on" allows them. Even if there are no orphans in practice, the claims are different.

So R04 changes a claim (from ruling out orphans to allowing them). This is a claim change declared as WORDING. FALLS.

Under this assumption, the wordings are equivalent. But the wordings make different claims (one rules out orphans explicitly, the other doesn't). A reader can't conclude "no orphans" from the new wording (it's compatible with orphans). So the reader can no longer conclude something the old allowed. This is a claim change.

Let me also think about whether R45 and R49 interact in a way that creates a problem.

R45 says: "A verdict that depends on one of these, or on the normative relation where a claim invokes worth, is a verdict given the input; where the input is missing, the verdict is unsettled." And "The semantics supplies no probability of truth, no merit function and no ranking of thinkers, and a claim that needs one is unsettled."

R49 says: "A case whose verdict turns on an input the case does not state, where the input is one of the declared inputs Part XIV lists or the normative relation, is a case with a missing input, not a refutation."

R45 says missing inputs → unsettled verdicts. R49 says missing inputs → not a refutation. These are consistent: if the input is missing, the verdict is unsettled (R45) and the case is not a refutation (R49).

But R45 also says "a claim that needs [probability/merit function/ranking] is unsettled." R49 says "a case whose verdict turns on an input the case does not state... is a case with a missing input, not a refutation." Does R49 apply to probability/merit function/ranking? These are NOT declared inputs (R45 says they're not supplied). They're also NOT the normative relation. So R49's rule (which applies to "declared inputs Part XIV lists or the normative relation") does NOT apply to probability/merit function/ranking.

So: if a case's verdict turns on a probability of truth (not a declared input, not N), R45 says the verdict is unsettled, but R49 does NOT say it's "not a refutation." It might still be a refutation (if the theory can't handle the case because it lacks a probability of truth).

Is this a problem? R45 says the claim is unsettled (missing input). R49 says declared-input cases are not refutations. But probability/merit/ranking cases are NOT declared-input cases. So they might be refutations (even though the verdict is unsettled).

But if the verdict is unsettled (R45), the theory doesn't make a claim about the case. An unsettled verdict can't refute anything (the theory is silent). So even without R49, an unsettled verdict is not a refutation. R49 just makes this explicit for declared inputs.

So the gap (R49 not covering probability/merit/ranking) is not a problem: unsettled verdicts are inherently non-refuting. R49 is just a clarification for declared inputs.

But is there a case where a verdict turns on a probability of truth and the theory's silence IS seen as a refutation? If the fixed verdict says "X" and the theory says "unsettled," the theory disagrees with the fixed verdict (is silent where the verdict is positive). This could be seen as a refutation (the theory can't handle the case). But R49 says (for declared inputs) this is "a missing input, not a refutation." For probability/merit/ranking, R49 doesn't say this. So the case might be a refutation.

Hmm, but R45 says the verdict is "unsettled" — which is a clear output (the theory says "we can't determine this without the input"). An unsettled output is not a refutation (it's a limitation, not a failure). R49 makes this explicit for declared inputs. For other missing inputs (probability, etc.), R45 says "unsettled" but R49 doesn't explicitly say "not a refutation." But the logic is the same: unsettled = not a refutation.

So there's a gap in R49's explicit coverage (it mentions only declared inputs and N, not probability/merit/ranking). But the logic extends: any unsettled verdict is not a refutation. R49 is just explicit about the main cases.

Is this a coherence issue? R49's new wording says "where the input is one of the declared inputs Part XIV lists or the normative relation." This is a restriction. But the principle (unsettled = not a refutation) should apply more broadly (to any missing input, including probability/merit/ranking). R49's restriction is narrower than the principle.

But R45 already says probability/merit/ranking claims are "unsettled." And unsettled is inherently non-refuting. So R49's restriction doesn't create a problem — it's just narrower than necessary.

Is this a coherence concern? R49 explicitly lists "declared inputs or the normative relation" but not "probability of truth, merit function, or ranking of thinkers." The latter are treated by R45 as "unsettled" (not as "missing inputs"). So R49's rule (missing input → not a refutation) doesn't apply to them. But they're "unsettled" (R45), which is inherently non-refuting. So the outcome is the same.

But the classification differs: declared inputs and N are "missing inputs" (R49), while probability/merit/ranking are "unsettled" (R45, not "missing inputs"). This classification difference might matter: R49 says missing inputs are not refutations. R45 says probability/merit/ranking are unsettled. Are "unsettled" and "not a refutation" the same? They should be (an unsettled verdict doesn't refute). But R49's explicit rule covers only missing inputs, not all unsettled cases.

Hmm, this is a subtle distinction. I think it's fine — the principle is clear (unsettled = non-refuting), and R49 is just explicit about the main cases. The gap is theoretical, not practical.

**Point 2: R39 (task b) — WORDING change alters the classification of N.**
The change "declared as a substantive input" → "taken as a substantive input" removes the implication that N is a declared input. In the text's terminology, "declared" is a loaded term ("declared inputs" in Part XIV, "declared indices" in Part 0/XIV). "Declared as" places N in the declared-input category; "taken as" does not. This reclassification (from declared input to primitive taken as input) is a claim change, even though it aligns with R38. R39 is declared WORDING (no claim change), but it changes a claim. Suggested repair: classify R39 as CLAIM (with declaration "N is now 'taken as' rather than 'declared as' a substantive input, consistent with its status as primitive 2"), or merge R39 into R38's change.

Hmm, wait. Let me reconsider R39 one more time. I want to make sure it really is a claim change.

The sentence in question (after R38 and R39 are applied): "and \(\mathcal N\subseteq A\times K\times\mathcal Rsn\times\mathcal V_A\) taken as a substantive input when aesthetic value is claimed."

The old version: "and \(\mathcal N\subseteq A\times K\times\mathcal Rsn\times\mathcal V_A\) declared as a substantive input when aesthetic value is claimed."

R39 changes "declared as" to "taken as." The question is: does this change what the text claims?

"Declared as a substantive input" — N is declared (by the modeller) to be a substantive input. The word "declared" means the modeller provides N and states its role.

"Taken as a substantive input" — N is taken (by the semantics) to be a substantive input. The word "taken" means the semantics accepts N as an input.

In the text's framework:
- "Declared" is associated with "declared inputs" (Part XIV: "stated inputs that the semantics records and does not supply") and "declared indices" and "declared provenance."
- "Taken" is used for N in Part XIV: "It is taken as an input and never derived."

So "declared as" suggests N is a declared input (a specific category). "Taken as" suggests N is an input taken by the semantics (which could be a primitive or a declared input).

The change from "declared" to "taken" moves N out of the "declared input" category. This is a classification change. A reader can conclude from the old wording: "N is a declared input." From the new wording: "N is an input taken by the semantics (possibly a primitive)." These are different conclusions.

So yes, R39 changes a claim (the classification of N). Even though it's a one-word change, it has classificatory weight in the text's terminology. R39 FALLS.

From the new wording ("taken as a substantive input"): a reader can no longer conclude N is a declared input (it's now "taken as," not "declared as"). So the reader can no longer conclude something the old allowed. → Claim change.

Actually, one more micro-consideration. The task says for WORDING changes: "the new wording says what the old wording said, in other words." If "declared as a substantive input" and "taken as a substantive input" say the same thing "in other words," then R39 is correctly classified as WORDING. The question is: do they say the same thing?

"Declared as X" = "asserted/announced as X." "Taken as X" = "accepted/used as X." In some contexts, these overlap (declaring something as X and taking it as X can be the same act). But in the text's terminology, "declared" is a specific act (the modeller states it) and "taken" is a different act (the semantics uses it). These are not the same "in other words."

So R39 does NOT say what the old said "in other words." It says something different (classification change). Not WORDING.

Actually, I realize I should double check one thing about R04. Let me re-examine whether the old and new wording are really different claims.

Old: "No predicate meaning 'really explains', 'is a cause' or 'is knowledge' appears anywhere (Derivation 6)."

Hmm, "appears anywhere" — appears anywhere in what? In the theory? In the document? In the semantics?

If "appears anywhere" means "appears anywhere in the document," then the claim is: these phrases never appear in the text. But they DO appear (e.g., in Part 0's grievances, Part II's discussion). So this reading makes the old wording literally false. The old wording must mean "appears anywhere in the theory's formal apparatus" (primitives, definitions, derived predicates). Under this reading: no predicate meaning X is part of the theory.

New: "No predicate meaning 'really explains', 'is a cause' or 'is knowledge' is taken as primitive, and no definition depends on one."

This says: no such predicate is primitive, and no definition uses one. But a derived predicate (defined by some definition) that no other definition uses would satisfy both conditions. So the new wording allows derived, unused predicates.

Is this a real difference? In the theory's construction, are there derived predicates that no definition uses? If every derived predicate is used somewhere (in another definition or in a membership condition), then "not primitive and not used" = "doesn't exist." But the new wording doesn't guarantee this — it's compatible with orphan definitions.

So the new wording is weaker. It allows orphans; the old doesn't. This is a claim change.

But here's a counterargument: "no definition depends on one" might mean "no definition at all depends on one" — including the definition that DEFINES it. If a definition defines "explains," that definition "depends on" the concept of "explains" (it needs to know what it's defining). Under this reading, "no definition depends on one" means no definition defines or uses one. This would make "not primitive and not depended on" = "not primitive and not defined and not used" = "doesn't exist."

But "depends on" typically means "requires as input" or "uses." A definition that DEFINES X depends on X's definiens (the right-hand side), not on X itself (the left-hand side). So "no definition depends on one" means no definition USES one, not no definition DEFINES one.

Unless "depends on" is read broadly as "involves." If "no definition depends on one" = "no definition involves one" (neither defines nor uses), then the two wordings are equivalent. But "depends on" more naturally means "requires" (uses as input), not "involves."

I think the standard reading makes the new wording weaker. So it's a claim change. R04 FALLS.

OK, truly truly final. Let me write.

Wait, I want to also think about whether there's a reading of "appears anywhere" that makes it equivalent to the new wording. "Appears anywhere" — if it means "appears as a primitive or in any definition," then "appears nowhere" = "not primitive and not in any definition." And "not primitive and no definition depends on one" = "not primitive and not used in any definition." "Not in any definition" vs. "not used in any definition" — a predicate can be DEFINED (in a definition as the definiendum) without being USED (in another definition's definiens). "Not in any definition" rules out both; "not used in any definition" only rules out being used. So they're different.

But if "appears in a definition" includes both being defined and being used, then "appears nowhere" = "not defined and not used and not primitive." And "not primitive and not used" allows being defined (orphan). So they're different.

OK, the difference is real. R04 FALLS.

Derivation 6's Claim: "Every predicate in Parts II–XIII is defined from Θ and, where invoked, N, together with declared indices and declared inputs." This supports "not taken as primitive" (every predicate is derived, so none is primitive except Θ and N which don't mean X).

Derivation 6's Consequence: "There is no residual predicate meaning 'explains', 'represents', 'is a cause', or 'is knowledge'." If "residual" means "present in the theory" (strong reading), this supports "appears nowhere" (old wording). If "residual" means "underived" (weak reading), this supports "not primitive" (part of new wording).

"no definition depends on one" — where is this supported? Part XIV: "Nothing depends on a predicate meaning 'really explains', 'is a cause', or 'is knowledge'." This supports "no definition depends on one." But Derivation 6 doesn't say this. Derivation 6's Consequence says "no residual predicate" (which might or might not entail "no definition depends on one").

If "no residual predicate" means "no such predicate exists," then it entails "no definition depends on one" (if the predicate doesn't exist, no definition can depend on it). Under this reading, Derivation 6 supports both parts of the new wording.

But if "no residual predicate" means "no underived predicate," it doesn't entail "no definition depends on one" (a derived predicate could be used by definitions). Under this reading, Derivation 6 supports only "not primitive."

The ambiguity of "residual" affects whether the pointer is imprecise. Under the strong reading, the pointer is fine (Derivation 6 supports the new wording). Under the weak reading, the pointer is imprecise (Derivation 6 supports only part of the new wording).

But the old wording ("appears nowhere") is supported under the strong reading of "residual." So the strong reading supports the old wording (which is stronger than the new). This means: under the strong reading, R04's new wording is weaker than what Derivation 6 shows. Under the weak reading, the pointer is imprecise.

Either way, there's a problem:
- Strong reading: R04 weakens the claim (Derivation 6 shows more).
- Weak reading: The pointer to Derivation 6 doesn't support "no definition depends on one."

I'll mention both.
